```text
1	We evaluate finer-grained 5-class depth classification (gen-0 through gen-4) using the same 7-feature ensemble and LOGO protocol described in \S\ref{sec:method}.
2	
3	\begin{table}[h]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
7	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
8	\toprule
9	\textbf{Held-out Generator} & \textbf{Macro F1} & \textbf{Weighted F1} & \textbf{Accuracy} \\
10	\midrule
11	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.484 & 0.506 & 0.524 \\
12	Pythia-1B        & 0.543 & 0.568 & 0.575 \\
13	Pythia-2.8B      & 0.523 & 0.540 & 0.560 \\
14	Qwen3-0.6B      & 0.513 & 0.521 & 0.546 \\
15	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.510 & 0.536 & 0.548 \\
16	Qwen3-8B        & 0.434 & 0.455 & 0.493 \\
17	\midrule
18	\textbf{Average} & \textbf{0.501 $\pm$ 0.038} & \textbf{0.521} & \textbf{0.541} \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}}
21	\caption{5-class LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out one generator for testing, training on the remaining five.}
22	\label{tab:5class-logo}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\begin{table}[h]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
29	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
30	\toprule
31	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Gen-0} & \textbf{Gen-1} & \textbf{Gen-2} & \textbf{Gen-3} & \textbf{Gen-4} \\
32	\midrule
33	Avg F1      & 0.921 & 0.634 & 0.268 & 0.259 & 0.423 \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}}
36	\caption{Per-class F1 averaged across all six LOGO folds.}
37	\label{tab:5class-perclass}
38	\end{table}
39	
40	\paragraph{Analysis.}
41	Moving from 3-class (gen-0 / gen-1 / gen-2+) to 5-class classification reduces LOGO macro F1 to approximately 0.50 (six-generator subset; the 3-class baseline for this subset is comparable to the 8-generator result of 0.762 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
42	The degradation is concentrated in the middle depths: gen-2 and gen-3 achieve only 0.268 and 0.259 F1, respectively.
43	The aggregated confusion matrix reveals that 36.5\% of true gen-2 samples are misclassified as gen-1, while 37.8\% of true gen-3 samples are predicted as gen-4.
44	Adjacent depths (gen-2$\leftrightarrow$gen-3, gen-3$\leftrightarrow$gen-4) account for the majority of errors, consistent with the intuition that deeper recursive generations converge in surface-level statistical signatures.
45	Gen-0 (human text) remains highly distinguishable (F1 = 0.921), and gen-1 retains moderate separability (F1 = 0.634).
46	Shape features that are near-stationary in the 3-class setting become substantially more important under 5-class evaluation: permutation importance of entropy\_std increases by 93\% and tail\_decay by 400\% relative to 3-class, yet these gains are insufficient to resolve adjacent depths.
47	These results support the 3-class grouping adopted in the main paper: collapsing gen-2+ preserves the most informative boundary (human vs.\ first-generation vs.\ deeper recursion) while avoiding the noise introduced by near-identical adjacent depths.
48	
49	\paragraph{Classifier comparison.}
50	To verify that 5-class degradation reflects feature-level limitations rather than classifier choice, we evaluate four alternatives under the same protocol: HistGradientBoosting (macro F1\,=\,0.501, 95\% bootstrap CI [0.496, 0.506]), random forest (0.476), logistic regression (0.447), and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction (0.451).
51	All classifiers perform comparably to the default XGBoost ensemble (0.501), ruling out classifier capacity as the bottleneck.
52	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary is the primary failure mode across all methods: these two classes are effectively inseparable with the current feature set. This establishes 3-class granularity as the practical ceiling for surface and entropy features.
53	At the 3-class level, ordinal-specific methods (CORAL ordinal regression, Frank-Hall reduction) similarly fail to improve over multiclass XGBoost (\S\ref{sec:results}), which confirms that the bottleneck is feature separability across all granularities, not the modeling of label ordinality.
54	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01G83KyqS6dwayG3cicpMa85`</summary>

```text
1	Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?
2	We fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:downstream}).
3	
4	\begin{table}[h]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\begin{tabular}{lrc}
8	\toprule
9	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{Test PPL} \\
10	\midrule
11	All depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
12	Size-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
13	Depth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
14	Binary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
15	Depth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
16	\bottomrule
17	\end{tabular}
18	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}
19	\label{tab:downstream}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	The results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.
23	Training on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).
24	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).
25	Cross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, which confirms the robustness of this null result.
26	
27	Restricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\,=\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.
28	
29	\paragraph{Equivalence testing.}
30	We apply a two one-sided $t$-test (TOST) with a $\pm$2\% relative margin (equivalence bounds $\pm$0.047 on the ppl scale) to formally assess whether the depth-aware and random subsampling conditions produce practically indistinguishable perplexity.
31	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951): size-matched (random) ppl\,=\,2.367 vs.\ depth 0--1 ppl\,=\,2.387, $\Delta$\,=\,0.020.
32	TOST confirms equivalence ($p = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}$); the two curation strategies yield practically indistinguishable downstream quality within the $\pm$2\% margin.
33	Cross-seed standard deviation is below 0.001 for both variants.
34	
35	Comparing the full dataset ($n$\,=\,12{,}299, ppl\,=\,2.335) against the depth-filtered subset ($n$\,=\,3{,}951, ppl\,=\,2.387): TOST \emph{rejects} equivalence (minimum equivalence margin\,=\,0.053, exceeding the $\pm$2\% bound), which confirms that data quantity drives perplexity reduction beyond what depth-aware selection can recover.
36	
37	Knowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training; at fixed sample count, depth-aware curation is statistically equivalent to uninformed random subsampling.
38	
39	\subsection*{Margin Sensitivity Analysis}
40	
41	To assess the robustness of the equivalence conclusion to margin choice, we repeat the TOST procedure across multiple equivalence margins \cite{lakens_equivalence}, following the recommendation to report sensitivity across plausible bounds rather than relying on a single threshold (Table~\ref{tab:tost-margins}).
42	
43	\begin{table}[h]
44	\centering
45	\small
46	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
48	\toprule
49	\textbf{Comparison} & \textbf{Margin} & \textbf{Equiv?} & \textbf{TOST $p$} \\
50	\midrule
51	Full (12{,}299) vs.\ Random (3{,}951) & $\pm$1\% & No & 1.0 \\
52	                                        & $\pm$2\% & Yes & $1 \times 10^{-6}$ \\
53	                                        & $\pm$3\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
54	                                        & $\pm$5\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
55	\midrule
56	Full (12{,}299) vs.\ Depth 0--1 (3{,}951) & $\pm$1\% & No & 1.0 \\
57	                                            & $\pm$2\% & No & 1.0 \\
58	                                            & $\pm$3\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
59	                                            & $\pm$5\% & Yes & $< 10^{-10}$ \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}}
62	\caption{TOST equivalence testing across multiple margins. ``Equiv?'' indicates whether equivalence is established at $\alpha = 0.05$.}
63	\label{tab:tost-margins}
64	\end{table}
65	
66	The full dataset vs.\ random subsample comparison passes equivalence at the $\pm$2\% margin ($p = 10^{-6}$), while the full vs.\ depth 0--1 comparison requires a $\pm$3\% margin.
67	In absolute terms, perplexity differences are small: $\Delta$\,=\,0.032 (full vs.\ random, 1.4\% of baseline) and $\Delta$\,=\,0.052 (full vs.\ depth 0--1, 2.2\% of baseline).
68	
69	Cohen's $d$ values are 86.0 (full vs.\ random) and 119.7 (full vs.\ depth 0--1).
70	These appear large but are unreliable: with only $n$\,=\,3 seeds per condition, the pooled standard deviation is extremely small ($<$0.001), artificially inflating $d$.
71	The inflated effect sizes reflect low seed-level variance (confirming reproducibility) rather than practically meaningful perplexity differences.
72	The TOST framework with pre-specified relative margins provides a more appropriate assessment than Cohen's $d$ in this low-seed regime.
73	
```

```text
1	To assess whether depth signal extends beyond handcrafted features, we fine-tune a pretrained language model end-to-end as a depth classifier using QLoRA \cite{qlora}.
2	
3	\paragraph{Setup.}
4	We fine-tune Qwen3-0.6B-Base with LoRA rank 16, learning rate $2 \times 10^{-4}$, and 3 epochs, framing depth estimation as 5-class sequence classification (gen-0 through gen-4).\footnote{Evaluated on the original 6-generator subset; XGBoost 3-class F1\,=\,0.789. We expect 8-generator training to further improve given additional diversity; the comparison direction is preserved.}
5	For 3-class evaluation, we collapse predictions into gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ post hoc.
6	All results use the same LOGO protocol as the main experiments.
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{QLoRA end-to-end depth classification vs.\ XGBoost on handcrafted features, under LOGO evaluation.}
12	\label{tab:qlora}
13	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{5-class F1} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Per-class F1 (5-class)} \\
17	\midrule
18	XGBoost (7 features) & 0.501 $\pm$ 0.038 & 0.789 & --- \\
19	QLoRA (Qwen3-0.6B)   & 0.583 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.905 $\pm$ 0.003 & 0.999\,/\,0.796\,/\,0.413\,/\,0.259\,/\,0.447 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\paragraph{Results.}
25	QLoRA achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.003), a +11.6\,pp improvement over the XGBoost baseline (0.789).
26	On 5-class evaluation, QLoRA reaches F1\,=\,0.583 ($\pm$0.010) vs.\ 0.501 for XGBoost (+8.2\,pp), with the largest gains at gen-1 (0.796 vs.\ 0.634) and gen-2 (0.413 vs.\ 0.268).
27	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary remains the primary failure mode (gen-3 F1\,=\,0.259), consistent with the feature-level analysis in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
28	
29	\paragraph{Hyperparameter robustness.}
30	A second run with LoRA rank 32 and seed 123 yields 5-class F1\,=\,0.587 ($\pm$0.005) and 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.004), which confirms stability across hyperparameter and seed variation ($\Delta < 1$\,pp on both metrics).
31	
32	\paragraph{Implications.}
33	The substantial 3-class improvement demonstrates that raw token embeddings contain depth signal beyond what summary statistics capture; the seven handcrafted features are a strong but lossy compression of the full distributional information.
34	End-to-end fine-tuning exploits token-level patterns (e.g., positional regularities, n-gram repetitions) that aggregate features cannot represent.
35	This confirms that the depth signal documented in our main analysis is real and exploitable beyond engineered features, while the handcrafted approach retains practical advantages: interpretability, computational efficiency (no GPU inference), and the ability to reveal the differential depth response pattern that motivates the task formulation.
36	
```

```text
1	We assess robustness to post-generation text modification by randomly replacing words at varying rates and re-evaluating the 8-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:human-edit}).
2	Random word replacement represents a worst-case perturbation: real human editing preserves semantic coherence, so practical degradation is expected to be substantially lower than these estimates.
3	
4	\begin{table}[h]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Robustness to random word replacement under 8-gen LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Bootstrap 95\% CIs on binary F1 ($n$\,=\,1000).}
8	\label{tab:human-edit}
9	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Perturbation rate} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-class F1} \\
13	\midrule
14	0\% (baseline) & 0.695 & [0.680, 0.710] & 0.737 \\
15	5\%            & 0.650 & [0.635, 0.666] & 0.641 \\
16	10\%           & 0.614 & [0.599, 0.629] & 0.492 \\
17	20\%           & 0.591 & [0.576, 0.605] & 0.367 \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	Binary depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) degrades sublinearly: the first 5\% replacement causes the largest drop ($-$4.6\,pp), with diminishing marginal impact thereafter ($-$3.6\,pp for 5\%$\to$10\%, $-$2.3\,pp for 10\%$\to$20\%).
23	At 10\% replacement, binary F1 remains 0.614, well above the 0.50 random baseline, which indicates that depth signal survives moderate perturbation.
24	
25	Three-class classification is more sensitive (0.737$\to$0.367 at 20\%), because distinguishing gen-0 from gen-1 relies heavily on lexical diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) that are directly disrupted by word replacement.
26	The binary metric, which excludes gen-0, is inherently more robust since it depends primarily on reference-model entropy features distributed across many tokens.
27	
28	\paragraph{Feature subset degradation.}
29	Table~\ref{tab:feature-degrade} compares perturbation robustness of the full 7-feature ensemble against an entropy-only subset (5 reference-model features).
30	The higher unperturbed binary F1 of the entropy-only subset (0.833 vs.\ 0.695) reflects the strong discriminability of reference-model features for the coarse binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+); however, lexical features are essential for the finer-grained ordinal task (3-class F1: 0.737 vs.\ 0.688) and provide greater robustness under perturbation.
31	At 20\% replacement, the 7-feature ensemble surpasses entropy-only on binary F1 (0.591 vs.\ 0.569), despite starting from a lower baseline.
32	Random word replacement drastically alters per-token context and makes entropy features (computed over the full token sequence) extremely sensitive to noise.
33	Lexical features (TTR, hapax ratio), as document-level summary statistics, are more stable under per-word perturbation and act as stabilizers in the full ensemble.
34	The crossover confirms that degradation in the full feature set reflects genuine signal disruption rather than a lexical-feature artifact.
35	
36	\begin{table}[h]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{Feature subset degradation under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO). Entropy-only: 5 reference-model features; 7-feature: full ensemble including lexical features.}
40	\label{tab:feature-degrade}
41	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
42	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{Perturb.} & \textbf{Ent-only Bin} & \textbf{7-feat Bin} & \textbf{Ent-only 3-cl} & \textbf{7-feat 3-cl} \\
45	\midrule
46	0\%  & 0.833 & 0.695 & 0.688 & 0.737 \\
47	5\%  & 0.738 & 0.650 & 0.539 & 0.641 \\
48	10\% & 0.667 & 0.614 & 0.427 & 0.492 \\
49	20\% & 0.569 & 0.591 & 0.314 & 0.367 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}}
52	\end{table}
53	
54	\paragraph{Baseline comparison.}
55	Table~\ref{tab:baseline-robustness} compares robustness across detection methods under identical perturbation conditions (8-gen LOGO, binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
56	
57	\begin{table}[h]
58	\centering
59	\small
60	\caption{Baseline robustness under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO, binary F1). Bold indicates highest F1 at each perturbation rate.}
61	\label{tab:baseline-robustness}
62	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
63	\toprule
64	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{0\%} & \textbf{5\%} & \textbf{10\%} & \textbf{20\%} \\
65	\midrule
66	7-feature ensemble & \textbf{0.695} & 0.650 & 0.614 & 0.591 \\
67	LD-Score           & 0.677 & \textbf{0.673} & \textbf{0.677} & \textbf{0.671} \\
68	Avg-LogProb        & 0.682 & 0.419 & 0.335 & 0.375 \\
69	FastDetectGPT      & 0.571 & 0.437 & 0.468 & 0.514 \\
70	Binoculars\textsuperscript{\dag}         & 0.500 & 0.461 & 0.446 & 0.425 \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}
73	
74	\vspace{2pt}
75	{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{\dag}Binoculars operates at chance level on this binary task; its degradation figures are not meaningful.}
76	\end{table}
77	
78	The 7-feature ensemble achieves the highest unperturbed binary F1 (0.695), but LD-Score surpasses it under perturbation due to near-zero degradation ($-$0.6\,pp across the full 0\%$\to$20\% range).
79	FastDetectGPT and Avg-LogProb exhibit non-monotonic degradation at higher perturbation rates, likely because severe perturbation introduces distributional artifacts that these token-level methods detect as anomalous.
80	Avg-LogProb is the most fragile overall ($-$34.7\,pp at 10\%), while FastDetectGPT drops sharply at 5\% ($-$13.4\,pp) before partially recovering.
81	LD-Score is nearly invariant to perturbation, consistent with its reliance on corpus-level distributional divergence rather than per-token likelihoods.
82	The 7-feature ensemble offers the highest starting performance among single-reference methods, with sublinear degradation ($-$10.4\,pp at 20\%).
83	
84	\paragraph{LLM-simulated sentence regeneration.}
85	To test robustness under semantically coherent editing, we use Qwen3-0.6B-Base to randomly regenerate 1--3 sentences per passage (avg 1.69 sentences, ${\sim}$50\% edit distance) and re-evaluate the 8-gen LOGO pipeline.
86	Binary F1 drops from 0.692 to 0.647 ($-$4.5\,pp, 6.5\% relative); 3-class F1 drops from 0.739 to 0.667 ($-$7.1\,pp, 9.7\% relative).
87	The binary degradation ($-$4.5\,pp) is nearly identical to that caused by 5\% random word replacement ($-$4.6\,pp), despite the LLM rephrase altering ${\sim}$50\% of characters.
88	This asymmetry arises because LLM-generated replacements preserve the underlying statistical signature of the generation process (entropy dynamics, depth-accumulated distributional drift), whereas random word substitution directly destroys local statistical signals.
89	Depth profiling thus captures deep generative structure that survives semantic-preserving edits.
90	
91	\paragraph{Seed complexity.}
92	Performance is stable across seed text complexity: stratifying seeds by vocabulary richness (TTR terciles) yields maximum F1 variation of 1.1\,pp across groups.
93	
```

```text
1	To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,
2	we repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest
3	in place of XGBoost (Table~\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).
4	All other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are
5	kept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.
6	
7	\begin{table}[h]
8	\centering
9	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}
10	\label{tab:alt-classifier}
11	\small
12	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
13	\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}
14	\toprule
15	Classifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\
16	\midrule
17	XGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\
18	Random Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\
19	Logistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	Both tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\approx 0.79$,
25	and even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for
26	three classes.
27	The per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers
28	(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),
29	confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,
30	not in classifier capacity.
31	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, which confirms that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.
32	
33	\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}
34	\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}
35	
36	To assess robustness to decoding temperature, we regenerate continuation chains at $t \in \{0.5, 0.9\}$ with fixed nucleus $p{=}0.9$ (matching the default) for four generators spanning three model families: Pythia-2.8B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, and Qwen3-0.6B. Evaluation uses the same LOGO protocol as the main experiments (Table~\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).
37	
38	\begin{table}[h]
39	\centering
40	\caption{Depth classification under varying temperature (fixed top-$p$\,=\,0.9). Binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+. LOGO evaluation; 95\% bootstrap CIs.$^{\ast}$}
41	\label{tab:sampling-ablation}
42	\small
43	\begin{tabular}{llcc c}
44	\toprule
45	Model & Temp & LOGO Binary F1 & 95\% CI & $\rho$(ent.\ mean) \\
46	\midrule
47	Pythia-2.8B & 0.5  & \textbf{0.814} & [.798, .828] & $-0.58$ \\
48	Pythia-2.8B & 0.7  & 0.740 & [.707, .742] & $-0.60$ \\
49	Pythia-2.8B & 0.9  & 0.480 & [.465, .498] & $-0.30$ \\
50	\midrule
51	OLMo-2-1B & 0.5  & \textbf{0.783} & [.768, .798] & $-0.72$ \\
52	OLMo-2-1B & 0.7  & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & $-0.60$ \\
53	OLMo-2-1B & 0.9  & 0.453 & [.437, .469] & $-0.26$ \\
54	\midrule
55	Qwen3-1.7B & 0.5  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\
56	Qwen3-1.7B & 0.7  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\
57	Qwen3-1.7B & 0.9  & 0.566 & --- & $-0.37$ \\
58	\midrule
59	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.5  & 0.711 & [.694, .729] & $-0.67$ \\
60	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.7  & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & $-0.60$ \\
61	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.9  & 0.598 & [.582, .615] & $-0.29$ \\
62	\bottomrule
63	\end{tabular}
64	\end{table}
65	\noindent{\footnotesize $^{\ast}$OLMo temperature experiments use OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release); the original OLMo-2-1B-hf weights used in the main experiments are no longer publicly available.}
66	
67	Temperature sensitivity is asymmetric and model-dependent. High-temperature degradation ($t{=}0.9$) is universal: all four generators lose $9.3$--$26.0$\,pp in binary F1, with entropy mean's $|\rho|$ weakening to $0.26$--$0.37$ (vs.\ $0.58$--$0.72$ at $t{=}0.5$). Low-temperature improvement ($t{=}0.5$) is model-dependent: Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B gain $+7.4$ and $+8.8$\,pp respectively, while both Qwen models show negligible change ($0$--$2.0$\,pp). This split tracks model family rather than scale: Qwen3-0.6B (smallest) and Qwen3-1.7B both plateau, while OLMo-2-1B (similar scale to Qwen3-1.7B) benefits substantially. One possible explanation is that Qwen models produce more peaked output distributions at $t{=}0.7$, leaving less room for low-temperature compression to sharpen the depth signal.
68	
69	The method operates reliably within $t \in [0.5, 0.7]$ across all tested models. Degradation onset at $t{=}0.9$ defines the practical upper bound, consistent with the hypothesis that elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that DDR exploits (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
70	
71	\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}
72	
73	Figure~\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.
74	Pythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.
75	The scale effect (larger models $\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.
76	
77	\begin{figure*}[ht]
78	\centering
79	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}
80	\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}
81	\label{fig:per-gen}
82	\end{figure*}
83
```

```text
abstract.tex:1:% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains

main.tex:1:% EMNLP 2026 ARR Submission — ACL 2023 Template
=== EN-DASH ===
=== ING-COMMA ===
abstract.tex:7:The depth signal degrades substantially under instruction tuning, which restricts current applicability to base models.
appendix_domain.tex:30:In the cross-domain setting, 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65) falls below binary F1 (0.74--0.75), reversing the pattern observed in the main experiment (3-class 0.762 $>$ binary 0.694).
appendix_feature_distributions.tex:10:\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Black line: pooled mean ($\pm$1 std shading) across all generators. Colored traces: individual generators. Features are labeled by DDR category (drifting, intermediate, near-stationary). Spearman $\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}
appendix_finer_depth.tex:21:\caption{5-class LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out one generator for testing, training on the remaining five.}
appendix_human_edit.tex:85:To test robustness under semantically coherent editing, we use Qwen3-0.6B-Base to randomly regenerate 1--3 sentences per passage (avg 1.69 sentences, ${\sim}$50\% edit distance) and re-evaluate the 8-gen LOGO pipeline.
related_work.tex:13:Our contribution is not a new detection method but the task formulation (generation depth classification, framed within the broader ordinal classification literature \cite{gutierrez_ordinal_survey,coral_ordinal,corn_ordinal}) and the finding that combining drifting, intermediate, and near-stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance.
related_work.tex:24:Iterative self-refinement \cite{self_refine} represents another form of recursive LLM processing, but targets output improvement through feedback loops rather than open-ended continuation; the distributional drift we study arises specifically from unconstrained recursive generation.
related_work.tex:31:We differ in three respects: (i) we study \emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal; (ii) we use handcrafted features rather than model-internal representations, enabling the DDR decomposition into drifting, intermediate, and near-stationary components; and (iii) while they characterize \emph{that} convergence occurs, our tri-partition reveals \emph{what} converges (location properties) versus what remains stable (distributional shape), a distinction with direct implications for detection.
related_work.tex:47:Scalable data curation relies on quality signals \cite{dsir,qurating,ccnet}, and recent work highlights the challenge of detecting training data contamination \cite{oren2024proving,golchin2024time} and maintaining data provenance at scale \cite{longpre2024data}.
results.tex:15:Under class balancing, macro F1 reaches 0.831 (per-class breakdown in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).
results.tex:22:Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control confirms the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
Shell cwd was reset to /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/.claude/sessions/review_reviewer_2_3f564e41
```

```text
The `acl.sty` itself hardcodes `a4paper`. This is actually the standard ACL 2023 template behavior -- recent ACL/EMNLP templates use A4. So this is NOT an issue; A4 is correct for ARR submissions.

Now let me compile my complete findings.

Here is the complete visual/formatting review of all 29 pages:

---

## Page Structure Summary

| Section | Pages |
|---------|-------|
| **Title + Abstract + Main text** (Sections 1-6, Limitations, Ethics Statement) | Pages 1-9 (main text ends partway through page 9) |
| **References** | Pages 10-13 (starts on page 10, with remaining references + Table 3 on page 13) |
| **Appendix** (Sections A-T) | Pages 13-29 (starts mid-page 13 with "A Dataset Statistics") |

**Main text page count**: The main body (including Limitations and Ethics Statement) ends on page 9. The Limitations section begins near the bottom of page 8 and the Ethics Statement concludes on page 9. This is within the 8-page limit since ACL/EMNLP allows Limitations and Ethics sections to extend beyond page 8 without counting toward the limit.

## Anonymity

**Maintained.** Page 1 shows "Anonymous ACL submission" with no author names. No self-identifying information observed throughout the paper.

## Line Numbers

**Present** on all pages (visible on the left margin of every page reviewed, numbered sequentially). This is correct for the `[review]` mode.

## Format Compliance

- **Double-column layout**: Correctly used throughout the main text (pages 1-9) and most appendix pages.
- **Single-column pages**: Pages 21, 22, 23, 24, 25 use full-width figures (violin plots, confusion matrices, UMAP plots, cross-temperature figures). This is acceptable for figure pages in the appendix.
- **Paper size**: A4 (595.276 x 841.89 pts), which is correct -- the official `acl.sty` hardcodes `a4paper`.
- **Font**: Times New Roman, 11pt -- correct per ACL template.

## Visual Issues Found

1. **Page 24 -- Stray "chance" text below Figure 9 caption**: Below the Figure 9 caption, there is a small isolated word "chance" floating alone. This appears to be a leftover LaTeX artifact (likely from a stray footnote marker or incomplete edit). This is a clear formatting bug.

2. **Page 22 -- Large whitespace**: Page 22 has substantial blank space below Figure 5 (confusion matrix) and between Figure 5 and Figure 6. The figures could be packed more tightly, though this is cosmetic in the appendix and not a compliance issue.

3. **Page 24 -- Excessive whitespace**: The bottom half of page 24 is empty below Figure 9's caption. This wastes space.

4. **Page 27 -- Mixed content density**: The top portion has Figure 8 (cross-temperature transfer, repeated from page 24 as a different rendering?), then transitions to Appendix P (Feature Ablation) and Q (Feature Correlation Matrix). The bottom half of the page is largely empty.

5. **Figures in main text (pages 6, 8)**: 
   - **Figure 1 (page 6)**: The bar chart and strip plot are readable but dense. The figure spans the right column. Labels are small but legible at 150 dpi.
   - **Figure 2 (page 8)**: LOGO binary F1 comparison chart. Readable, error bars visible. The text "Binary F1 (gen-1 vs. gen-2+)" in the figure is quite small.
   - **Table 1 (page 6)**: Clearly formatted, numbers aligned, bold for best results visible.
   - **Table 2 (page 7)**: Clear and readable.

6. **No overlapping text or cut-off elements** detected on any page.

7. **All table captions are self-contained** and positioned correctly above tables.

8. **All figure captions** are positioned below figures as expected.

## Summary of Findings

- **Main text ends**: Page 9 (Limitations starts page 8, Ethics Statement ends page 9) -- **compliant** with 8-page limit (Limitations/Ethics don't count)
- **References start**: Page 10
- **Appendix starts**: Page 13 (mid-page, after remaining references and Table 3)
- **Total pages**: 29
- **Anonymity**: Maintained throughout
- **Line numbers**: Present on all pages
- **Page size**: A4, correct per ACL template
- **Critical visual bug**: Stray "chance" text on page 24 below Figure 9
- **Minor issues**: Excessive whitespace on pages 22, 24, 27 (appendix only, not blocking)
- **No overlapping/cut-off/unreadable elements** detected
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the information I need to compile a thorough review. Let me also read a few more appendix files to complete my understanding, and check some specific details.

Let me summarize what I know:

**Paper Summary:**
- Title: "Depth Profiling: Classifying Recursive Generation Depth in Base LLM Text Chains"
- Task: Generation depth classification (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+) instead of binary real-vs-synthetic detection
- Key finding: Differential Depth Response (DDR) - a tri-partition of features into drifting, intermediate, and near-stationary


- Method: 7 handcrafted features combining reference-model likelihood statistics and surface-level lexical diversity, trained with XGBoost
- Results: Leak-free binary F1 scores of 0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation and 0.764 on cross-model chains
- Scope: Focuses on base models only, with instruction tuning substantially degrading the signal; evaluated across 8 generators from 5 families ranging from 0.6B to 8B parameters on a 37K text dataset
- Format: 29 pages total with main text ending at page 9, compliant with submission requirements

; includes anonymous submission, line numbers, ACL template with review option, and dedicated limitations section
- References: 67 bibliography entries with 89 citations, all accounted for; 7 entries not found in OpenAlex (DetectGPT, XGBoost, Pythia—likely API lookup issues) and 5 minor field mismatches
- Appendix spans roughly 20 sections across pages 13-29
- AI writing markers: Em-dashes appear only in LaTeX comments, not in rendered text; no en-dashes detected

; some -ing comma patterns present but appear legitimate rather than AI-generated

The core contributions are generation depth classification (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+) and the DDR tri-partition identifying drifting, intermediate, and near-stationary features, but the technical novelty is limited—it's essentially seven standard features fed into XGBoost, with DDR being more of an empirical observation that certain features behave differently across depths. The extension from binary detection to multi-class depth classification feels incremental, and the F1 score of 0.694 suggests moderate performance at best, while the restriction to base models only significantly constrains real-world applicability. Related work adequately covers Broken Telephone, Markovian Chains, and stylometry approaches.

The paper maintains consistent framing throughout—the abstract and introduction clearly establish the problem and list two contributions, the title aligns with the content, and limitations around base-model scope are transparently stated. The experimental design is solid with LOGO evaluation, multiple control conditions, and thorough ablation studies, plus honest acknowledgment of the moderate performance and scope constraints. Statistical rigor is evident through bootstrap confidence intervals and McNemar tests, though the related work could have included more on data contamination detection and text forensics.

The core weaknesses are more fundamental: the DDR finding that some features drift while others remain stable under recursive generation is fairly intuitive—NLL naturally decreases as text becomes more model-like, and declining type-token ratios align with known model collapse patterns. The tri-partition essentially characterizes which features change rather than uncovering something surprising. More critically, restricting the analysis to base models severely undermines practical relevance since instruction-tuned models dominate real-world LLM deployment, and the instruct experiments reveal dramatic performance drops that suggest the findings may not generalize.

The classification performance itself (F1 = 0.694) isn't compelling enough for practical use, and there's a fundamental framing problem: the paper claims empirical rather than methodological contributions, yet the DDR is mostly descriptive statistics and the classifier is just standard features with XGBoost. This creates tension about what the actual contribution is. With only two contributions—the DDR characterization and task formulation—the paper feels thin for a venue like EMNLP, especially since the task formulation alone doesn't carry sufficient weight.

The appendix is bloated with 20 sections that seem designed to preempt criticism rather than address core issues. More problematically, the downstream experiment actually shows that depth-aware curation doesn't improve training, which directly contradicts the practical motivation about data provenance. The "location vs shape" interpretation about recursive generation is presented as empirical observation but lacks theoretical grounding—the paper hints at softmax architecture and sampling parameters but doesn't rigorously explain why this pattern would emerge. There's also an inconsistency in Table 2 with the baseline comparisons that needs clarification.

The cross-model chains use a different generator subset than the LOGO experiments, making direct comparison difficult. Temperature sensitivity is a major practical limitation—the method degrades substantially at higher temperatures, which is a fundamental constraint on real-world applicability rather than just a minor limitation. The paper uses "entropy" as shorthand for NLL which could confuse readers, though overall the writing is clean and well-executed.

For the overall assessment, this is a solid empirical study with careful experimental design, but the core contribution—the DDR finding—is more descriptive than deeply insightful, and restricting the analysis to base models significantly limits practical impact. The gap between what the paper promises (characterizing generative provenance) and what it actually delivers (moderate-performance classification on base-model text only) is substantial. On soundness, the methodology is rigorous but some claims overstate the evidence, and on excitement, while the question is interesting, the limited novelty and narrow scope constrain the contribution.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RMjfg4FXYBusahKaPcKkew`</summary>

```text
1	% Appendix — Length Confound Analysis
2	% Data source: 8-generator LOGO corpus (37,058 samples)
3	
4	Surface diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) mechanically depend on text length:
5	shorter texts yield higher type-token ratios by construction.
6	If generation depth systematically shortens text, observed depth trends could be length artifacts rather than genuine distributional shifts.
7	We investigate with three complementary analyses.
8	
9	\paragraph{(a) Length--depth correlation.}
10	Spearman rank correlation between word count and generation depth is weak across all eight generators ($|\rho| < 0.14$); depth does not systematically shorten or lengthen text in our corpus.
11	
12	\paragraph{(b) Length--feature correlations.}
13	While length--depth correlation is weak, individual features show moderate length dependence:
14	tail\_decay ($|\rho| = 0.38$), hapax\_ratio ($|\rho| = 0.30$), and TTR ($|\rho| = 0.29$).
15	Reference-model features show weaker length correlations (entropy\_mean $|\rho| = 0.09$, entropy\_std $|\rho| = 0.18$).
16	This motivates the length-matched evaluation below: if length mediates some depth--feature association, controlling for length will reduce classification performance.
17	
18	\paragraph{(c) Length-matched classification.}
19	We subsample the dataset so that word-count distributions are equalized across depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+), then retrain the binary XGBoost classifier under pooled LOGO (8-fold).
20	Length-matched binary F1\,=\,0.724, down from 0.770 on the unmatched set ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.046).
21	To distinguish this from a sample-size effect, we run a random-subsample control at equal $N$ without length matching: F1\,=\,0.767, which confirms that the 0.046 drop reflects genuine length deconfounding rather than reduced data.
22	
23	\paragraph{Conclusion.}
24	Length accounts for approximately 6\% of discriminative performance ($\Delta$\,=\,0.046 out of 0.770).
25	The majority of the depth signal is length-independent: length-matched F1 (0.724) remains far above chance and significantly above all individual detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
26	Surface features (TTR, hapax\_ratio) partially co-vary with length, but their depth-discriminative contribution persists after length control, consistent with generation-induced vocabulary compression operating independently of text truncation.
27	
```

```text
1	We evaluate whether depth signal generalizes beyond Wikipedia by constructing generation chains from three additional domains: News articles, Academic abstracts, and Code documentation.
2	For each domain, we sample 200 seed texts and generate chains of depth 3 using three generators (Pythia-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, Mistral-7B).
3	The same 7-feature ensemble and LOGO protocol are applied.
4	
5	\begin{table}[h]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{Cross-domain depth classification under LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Three generators $\times$ 200 seeds per domain. Bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n$\,=\,1000).}
9	\label{tab:domain}
10	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-class F1} \\
13	\midrule
14	Code           & 0.754 & [0.737, 0.770] & 0.634 \\
15	News           & 0.746 & [0.726, 0.764] & 0.652 \\
16	Academic       & 0.739 & [0.720, 0.757] & 0.622 \\
17	\bottomrule
18	\end{tabular}
19	\end{table}
20	
21	All three non-Wikipedia domains achieve binary F1 $>$ 0.73 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, which demonstrates that depth signal is not an artifact of Wikipedia text properties.
22	
23	\paragraph{Domain $\times$ temperature interaction.}
24	Cross-domain generation used $t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}1.0$ (vs.\ the main experiment's $t{=}0.7$, top-$p{=}0.9$).
25	Supplementary evaluation at $t{=}0.7$ reveals a domain $\times$ temperature interaction: the main Wikipedia experiment favors lower temperature (depth signal degrades monotonically with rising temperature; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), while cross-domain evaluation favors higher temperature ($t{=}1.0$ yields +3 to +11\,pp over $t{=}0.7$; News 0.746 vs.\ 0.712, Academic 0.739 vs.\ 0.628, Code 0.754 vs.\ 0.687).
26	This asymmetry likely reflects reference-model calibration: Pythia-410M, trained predominantly on web text, detects subtle per-step drift in Wikipedia but requires larger distributional divergence (produced by higher temperature) to detect depth signal in out-of-distribution domains.
27	This finding suggests that reference model selection is a key factor for cross-domain deployment of depth profiling.
28	
29	\paragraph{3-class inversion as DDR evidence.}
30	In the cross-domain setting, 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65) falls below binary F1 (0.74--0.75), reversing the pattern observed in the main experiment (3-class 0.762 $>$ binary 0.694).
31	This inversion reflects degraded gen-0 vs.\ gen-1 separation: the Pythia-410M reference model is calibrated to Wikipedia text, so its baseline entropy estimates are less discriminative for out-of-domain human text and compresses the feature distance between gen-0 and gen-1.
32	The binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is unaffected because both classes are generated under identical domain and decoding conditions; recursive drift is a relative change that does not depend on absolute calibration.
33	This pattern provides additional evidence that DDR captures generation-process invariants rather than domain-specific text properties: depth signal remains robust even when the human-vs-machine boundary becomes less distinct.
34	
```

```text
1	We evaluate depth profiling under three prompt types using Pythia-2.8B: \emph{continuation} (``Continue the following text''), \emph{elaboration} (``Elaborate on the following text''), and \emph{inspired rewriting} (``Write a new passage inspired by the following text''). Each uses 995 seeds $\times$ 5 depths.
2	
3	\paragraph{Within-prompt-type classification.}
4	5-fold CV macro-F1: continuation 0.78, rewriting 0.74, elaboration 0.73. All three prompt types exhibit the same differential depth response pattern: drifting features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show consistent monotonic trends, while near-stationary features (entropy max, tail decay) change by less than 5\%.
5	
6	\paragraph{Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO).}
7	Leave-one-prompt-type-out evaluation yields average F1\,=\,0.753, which confirms that depth signal transfers across prompt types. Feature decay rates differ: continuation shows the strongest drift (16.9\% per depth step), followed by rewriting (6.1\%) and elaboration (1.4\%), consistent with continuation chains applying less semantic constraint on the output.
8
```

```text
1	\section{Per-Generator Equivalence Testing}
2	\label{app:tost}
3	
4	To confirm that the near-stationary classification is not a pooling artifact, we apply two one-sided $t$-tests (TOST) \cite{lakens_equivalence} for each feature within each generator separately (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, equivalence bound $|d| < 0.20$).
5	Table~\ref{tab:tost-per-gen} reports observed $|d|$; \checkmark{} marks features for which the TOST null hypothesis (non-equivalence) is rejected at $\alpha = 0.05$.
6	
7	Entropy min passes equivalence for all eight generators; entropy max passes for six of eight (exceeding $\varepsilon$ only for Pythia-1B and Pythia-2.8B, where $|d| \approx 0.21$--$0.25$); tail decay passes for four of eight.
8	All drifting and intermediate features fail equivalence uniformly, with $|d|$ ranging from 0.23 (entropy std, Mistral) to 1.40 (entropy mean, Pythia-1B).
9	The partition is robust at the individual-generator level, with entropy min as the most consistently stationary feature.
10	
11	\begin{table}[h]
12	\centering
13	\footnotesize
14	\caption{Per-generator TOST equivalence ($\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Observed $|d|$; \checkmark{} = equivalent ($p_{\mathrm{TOST}} < 0.05$). Features grouped by DDR partition.}

16	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
```

```text
1	\section{Independent-Seed Control}
2	\label{app:seed-control}
3	
4	To rule out topic-level leakage from shared seeds across generators, we construct an independent-seed evaluation where Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B use completely disjoint Wikipedia seed pools (500 seeds each, zero overlap).
5	Each generator produces depth-0 through depth-4 chains on its own seed pool; features are extracted with the same Pythia-410M reference model.
6	We then train a 7-feature XGBoost classifier on one generator's data and test on the other, in both directions.
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
12	\toprule
13	\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
14	\midrule
15	Pythia-2.8B $\rightarrow$ OLMo-2-1B & 0.695 & [0.673, 0.717] \\
16	OLMo-2-1B $\rightarrow$ Pythia-2.8B & 0.666 & [0.643, 0.689] \\
17	\midrule
18	Independent-seed average & 0.680 & --- \\
19	Shared-seed LOGO (reference) & 0.694 & [0.688, 0.700] \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\caption{Independent-seed cross-generator evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Disjoint seed pools eliminate any possibility of topic-based leakage. The average independent-seed F1 (0.680) is within 1.4\,pp of the shared-seed LOGO baseline (0.694), confirming that the classifier captures generative degradation patterns rather than seed-specific topic content.}
23	\label{tab:seed-control}
24	\end{table}
25	
26	The small gap ($-$1.4\,pp) between independent-seed and shared-seed evaluation falls within bootstrap confidence intervals, and both directions individually exceed 0.66.
27	Combined with the seed-level demeaning control (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), these results provide converging evidence that depth classification is driven by distributional properties of recursive generation, not topic identity.
28	
```

```text
1	We analyze the distribution and characteristics of binary classification errors (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) across the 8-generator LOGO evaluation ($N$\,=\,29{,}095 samples, overall error rate 28.08\%).
2	
3	\paragraph{Error asymmetry.}
4	Errors are heavily asymmetric: 86.2\% are false negatives (gen-2+ predicted as gen-1) and only 13.8\% are false positives (gen-1 predicted as gen-2+).
5	The classifier systematically under-predicts depth, consistent with the DDR pattern: shallow gen-2+ samples that have not yet accumulated sufficient distributional drift are indistinguishable from gen-1.
6	
7	\paragraph{Per-depth error rates.}
8	Error rates decrease monotonically with depth: depth-2 samples are the primary failure point (48.42\% error rate), followed by depth-3 (29.17\%) and depth-4 (20.32\%).
9	Depth-1 false positive rate is 7.11\%.
10	The monotonic decrease confirms that deeper generations accumulate progressively stronger depth signatures; the gen-1$\to$gen-2 transition is therefore the hardest to detect.
11	
12	\paragraph{Per-generator variation.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:error-by-gen} shows error rates vary substantially across generators, correlating with model scale: larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) produce text closer to gen-1 characteristics, which makes depth discrimination harder.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Binary classification error rates by generator (8-gen LOGO). Sorted by error rate descending.}
19	\label{tab:error-by-gen}
20	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
21	\toprule
22	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Samples} & \textbf{Errors} & \textbf{Error rate} \\
23	\midrule
24	Mistral-7B-v0.3 & Mistral & 3{,}853 & 1{,}734 & 45.00\% \\
25	Qwen3-8B        & Qwen    & 3{,}502 & 1{,}197 & 34.18\% \\
26	Llama-3.2-3B    & Llama   & 3{,}685 & 1{,}112 & 30.18\% \\
27	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 3{,}718 & 1{,}054 & 28.35\% \\
28	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 3{,}614 &   866 & 23.96\% \\
29	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 3{,}621 &   814 & 22.48\% \\
30	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 3{,}542 &   745 & 21.03\% \\
31	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 3{,}560 &   648 & 18.20\% \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	\paragraph{Feature-level explanation.}
37	Mann-Whitney U tests comparing correctly vs.\ incorrectly classified samples reveal that drifting features show significant distributional differences between correct and incorrect predictions: entropy\_mean ($p < 10^{-300}$), TTR ($p < 10^{-300}$), and hapax\_ratio ($p < 10^{-300}$).
38	In contrast, shape features show no discriminating power: entropy\_max ($p = 0.584$) and tail\_decay ($p = 0.653$).
39	Misclassified gen-2+ samples have systematically higher TTR (0.464 vs.\ 0.302 for correct) and entropy\_mean (2.26 vs.\ 1.57); these samples retain gen-1-like lexical diversity and have not undergone the distributional convergence characteristic of deeper generations.
40	This is consistent with the DDR pattern (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}): errors concentrate where depth-accumulated drift is insufficient, and shape features (which are near-stationary by design) provide no additional discriminative signal for error correction.
41	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a comprehensive understanding of the paper. Let me also check for some remaining details:

1. The appendix_prompt_type mentions binary F1 numbers (0.671 vs 0.749) for paraphrastic chains - let me check if these are consistent with what's reported elsewhere.

2. Let me check the appendix count. Looking at main.tex, the appendices are:
- A: Dataset Statistics
- B: Length Confound Analysis
- C: Prompt Type Generalization
- D: Finer-Grained Depth Classification
- E: End-to-End Depth Classification (QLoRA)


- F through S: Additional analyses including leave-one-feature-out, confusion matrices, classifier sensitivity, robustness tests, cross-domain generalization, error analysis, downstream utility, cross-model details, feature distributions, temperature transfer, feature ablation, correlation matrices, reference models, and seed control.
- T: TOST (Two One-Sided Tests)
- U: Qualitative Examples

Now I'm counting through the actual LaTeX structure to verify the total number of appendix sections by checking the input commands in main.tex.

Looking at the list, I count 21 appendix sections total, which seems like too many for a long paper at EMNLP. I should also verify the paraphrasing claim mentioned in the results about the weaker signal from paraphrastic chains compared to continuation prompts, and check what's actually covered in the prompt-type appendix section. I see the discrepancy now—the appendix reports macro-F1 scores from 5-fold cross-validation for three prompt types, while the results section presents binary F1 metrics that include paraphrastic chains, which aren't detailed in the appendix. The slight numerical differences (0.78 vs 0.781, etc.) likely reflect different evaluation approaches rather than rounding errors. I'm noticing the paraphrastic chain results cited in the main text (F1=0.671) don't actually appear anywhere in the appendix data—the appendix only covers continuation, rewriting, and elaboration prompt types. The binary F1 scores in the results section are also different from the macro-F1 scores in the appendix, which makes sense since they're measuring different things, but the paraphrastic chains themselves seem to be missing from the supporting materials entirely.

Now looking at the critical issues: the DDR framework's core contribution—dividing results into drifting, intermediate, and near-stationary categories—is really just a descriptive categorization of which statistics change under recursive generation. The findings that NLL decreases and vocabulary contracts are already well-established in the model collapse literature, so the paper lacks theoretical depth beyond organizing existing observations.

The intermediate category only contains one metric, which makes the tri-partition feel somewhat arbitrary. The authors themselves acknowledge this is purely empirical work with no new methods or theory, just a finer-grained characterization of known phenomena.

The restriction to base models is the paper's most critical limitation. The instruct-tuned results are stark—OLMo-Instruct's LOGO F1 drops dramatically to 0.338 compared to 0.763 for the base model, and entropy_mean's correlation even reverses sign. Since all deployed LLMs are instruction-tuned, this severely limits the practical relevance of the findings.

The contribution list itself is quite thin for an 8-page paper. The task formulation is just a straightforward extension from binary to three classes, and the DDR characterization, while the main claim, is descriptive rather than explanatory. The extensive appendix suggests the authors recognize this gap and are trying to compensate with additional material.

There's a critical issue in Appendix L: the downstream utility experiment actually shows that depth-aware curation doesn't improve training utility compared to random subsampling, which directly undermines the practical motivation laid out in the introduction. This finding deserves much more prominence than being buried in an appendix. Additionally, the paper inconsistently uses 5, 6, or 8 generators across different experiments, making it difficult to compare results across sections—the 5-generator cross-model F1 of 0.764 versus the 8-generator LOGO F1 of 0.694 discrepancy illustrates how these choices affect the numbers.

The method also shows substantial temperature sensitivity, degrading by 9.3 to 26 percentage points at t=0.9, which raises a fundamental question: if DDR is supposed to characterize an intrinsic property of recursive generation, the fact that the signal largely disappears at slightly higher temperatures suggests it may be more parameter-dependent than intrinsic. Finally, Table 2 reports a binary F1 of 0.705 while Table 1 shows 0.694 for the same method, and though the footnote explains this is due to pooled versus per-generator macro averaging, having two different metrics reported for the same baseline creates unnecessary confusion.

The "entropy" terminology is also problematic—the paper explicitly acknowledges using it as shorthand for per-token NLL rather than true Shannon entropy, yet this non-standard usage persists throughout figures, tables, and feature names like "entropy_mean," which will confuse readers familiar with information theory. Additionally, the paraphrastic chain results reported in the main text (binary F1 = 0.671) lack corresponding appendix support, as Appendix C only covers three prompt types without addressing paraphrastic chains specifically.

On the minor side, there are some formatting quirks like em-dashes in comments and stray text on page 24, though the paper is otherwise well-written. The authors do show good judgment acknowledging that Cohen's d values in the downstream experiment are meaningless given the low seed count (n=3), and the experimental design itself is solid with appropriate controls like LOGO, length matching, and perturbation robustness checks.

The statistical approach is rigorous—bootstrap confidence intervals, McNemar tests, TOST equivalence testing, and Bonferroni correction all strengthen the claims. What's particularly valuable is the transparency about limitations: they're honest about the base-model scope, moderate performance gains, and temperature sensitivity. The depth classification task itself is genuinely interesting, and the QLoRA experiment effectively demonstrates that the depth signal is real and learnable beyond just handcrafted features. The related work is comprehensive and positions their contribution fairly.

There are some reference gaps worth noting—more recent work on synthetic data detection in training pipelines could strengthen the recursive generation discussion, and the connection to test-time compute and chain-of-thought generation chains isn't explored. The stylometry section could also provide fuller context for MTLD and HD-D in the LLM detection literature.

The fundamental issue is that while the paper tackles an interesting question and executes the empirical study thoroughly, the core contribution is more descriptive than explanatory. The method itself—standard linguistic features fed into XGBoost—lacks technical novelty, and restricting the analysis to base models significantly limits real-world applicability. The negative result on downstream utility further undermines the practical case. The extensive appendix shows rigor, but it doesn't compensate for a contribution that documents a phenomenon without explaining its mechanisms or demonstrating practical value. Their own acknowledgment that the work is "empirical rather than methodological" is refreshingly honest, though it also pinpoints exactly where the paper falls short.

For EMNLP 2026 ARR, I'd rate it around 2.5 overall—borderline for Findings at best. The soundness is solid (3.5/5), but the excitement is limited (2.0/5) given the narrow scope and negative downstream results. The paper might work for Findings if they reframe the tension between the novel task formulation and the limited practical impact, and better justify why base models are the right scope. But I'm second-guessing myself here—the task itself is genuinely novel, the empirical evidence is thorough, and they're being transparent about limitations. The DDR decomposition, while descriptive, is a new characterization that hasn't been documented before. Maybe I'm being too harsh on the excitement score given that they're opening a new research direction, even if the immediate payoff is modest.

Still, the binary F1 of 0.694 isn't compelling, the downstream experiment failed, and restricting to base models is a significant limitation. I'll settle on 3.0 overall for Findings—the execution is solid enough and the question interesting enough to warrant acceptance there, even if the contribution feels thin for a main conference venue. I'm fairly confident in this assessment.

Now I'm ready to write and submit the full review.
```

本质上是一个统计分类——测量哪些特征变化、哪些不变——而非一个有理论支撑的发现。intermediate 类别仅有一个成员（entropy_std），进一步削弱了 partition 的结构性。论文自述 \"Our contributions are empirical rather than methodological\"（§1 第20行），这诚实但也准确指出了核心弱点。\n- 为什么影响接收：EMNLP 长论文需要足够的 intellectual contribution。当核心发现是描述性的且无理论解释时，即使实验再充分也难以达到 Conference 标准。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 提供 DDR tri-partition 的理论解释——为什么 location drift 而 shape stable？§5 仅用一句话 gesture at \"softmax architecture and fixed sampling parameters\"，这需要严格论证。(2) 或者将贡献重新定位为 benchmark/dataset paper，强调 evaluation suite 和 task formulation 的价值。\n\n**N2** [critical] Base-model-only scope 从根本上限制影响力\n\n- 具体位置：§1 第15行, §5 (Discussion) 第7-11行, §Limitations 第1-9行\n- 问题：所有 8 个 generator 均为 base (pretrained) models。Instruct 实验显示 DDR 信号在 instruction tuning 下急剧衰减：OLMo-2-1B-Instruct LOGO F1 从 0.763 降至 0.338，entropy_mean 的 ρ 从 −0.6 反转至 +0.48。实际部署的 LLM 文本几乎全部来自 instruction-tuned models。论文将 base model 研究定位为 \"isolating the generation mechanism\"，但如果 mechanism 在 instruction tuning 下质性改变（6/7 特征方向反转），那么 base model 上的发现可能无法泛化，削弱了 DDR 作为 \"recursive generation 的内在属性\" 的定位。\n- 为什么影响接收：实用相关性是 EMNLP 的重要评判标准。一个仅在 base model 上有效的方法/发现，其受众和影响力非常有限。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 提供至少 2-3 个 instruct model 的系统性实验（不仅是 Limitations 中的初步结果），分析 DDR 在 instruct 下为何失效。(2) 若无法扩展到 instruct，将论文明确定位为面向 pretraining data pipeline 的 quality signal（而非通用 provenance 工具），并在 Introduction 动机中收窄 scope。\n\n**N3** [critical] 贡献列表对长论文过于单薄\n\n- 具体位置：§1 第20-24行（contribution list）\n- 问题：仅列出 2 条贡献：(1) task formulation + evaluation suite, (2) DDR characterization。对于一篇 8 页正文 + 16 页附录的长论文，两条贡献不足以支撑篇幅。Task formulation（3-class instead of binary）是直接的扩展；DDR 如 N1 所述是描述性发现。相比之下，QLoRA 实验（Appendix E）显示 3-class F1 从 0.789 跃升至 0.905（+11.6 pp），证明 token-level 表示中存在更丰富的 depth signal，但这个结果被埋在附录中。\n- 为什么影响接收：审稿人和 AC 会评估 short paper。\n\n---\n\n## Should-Fix 问题（影响评分但不一票否决）\n\n**W1** [warning] Downstream utility 实验结果为负，削弱 Introduction 的 practical motivation\n\n- 具体位置：Appendix L (Downstream Utility), §1 第7行 (\"such text 0-1 ppl 2.387）。这一负面结果直接否定了\"depth classification 对 data curation 有价值\"的隐含承诺。该结果被埋在附录中，正文仅用一句话提及（§4.3 最后一行：\"Preliminary downstream evaluation finds that depth composition does not determine training utility at fixed sample count\"）。\n- 建议：(1) 在正文 Discussion 中更显著地讨论这一负面结果及其含义。(2) 调整 Introduction 动机，弱化 data curation 方面的暗示。\n\n**W2** [warning] 实验中 generator subset 不一致造成混乱\n\n- 具体位置：§3.4 第76-77行, Table 1 footnote, Appendix E (QLoRA), main.tex 第40-41行\n- 问题：不同实验使用 5/6/8 个 generators：main LOGO 用 8 个，cross-model chains 用 5 个，QLoRA 用 6 个，部分 appendix 用 6 个。main.tex 第40-41行的 appendix note 试图澄清但增加了复杂性。Cross-model binary F1 = 0.764（5-gen）vs. LOGO binary F1 = 0.694（8-gen）的比较因 subset 不同而难以解读。Table 1 (0.694) vs. Table 2 (0.705) 的 7-feature binary F1 差异（pooled vs. macro average）进一步增加困惑。\n- 建议：(1) 统一核心实验使用 8-gen subset（或标明这不可能的原因）。(2) 在一个集中的表格中列出每个实验使用的 generator subset。(3) Table 1 和 Table 2 应使用相同的 aggregation 方式。\n\n**W3** [warning] 温度敏感性暗示 DDR 可能是参数依赖而非内在属性\n\n- 具体位置：§4.2 (Temperature sensitivity), Appendix H (Sampling Sensitivity)\n- 问题：所有 4 个测试 generator 在 t=0.9 时损失 9.3-26.0 pp binary F1。论文将 DDR 描述为 \"consistent with recursive generation shifting the NLL location while distributional shape remains stable\"（§3.3 第66行），这暗示 DDR 是 recursive generation 的内在属性。但 t=0.9 时信号大幅衰减说明 DDR 的强度高度依赖 sampling parameters，削弱了 \"intrinsic\" 的定位。\n- 建议：在 Discussion 中明确区分 DDR 作为现象（parameter-dependent）和 DDR 作为 property of recursive generation（intrinsic）。目前措辞倾向后者但证据只支持前者。\n\n**W4** [warning] Paraphrastic chain 结果在附录中缺乏支撑\n\n- 具体位置：§4.2 第88行 (\"Paraphrastic chains yield prompt type，未包含 paraphrastic chain 的详细方法和结果。该数字在正文中无完整的实验支撑。\n- 建议：将 paraphrastic chain 实验纳入 Appendix C 并提供完整的实验细节。\n\n**W5** [warning] \"Entropy\" 术语使用造成持续混淆\n\n- 具体位置：§3.3 第42行 (\"We use 'entropy' as shorthand for token-level NLL\")\n- 问题：所有特征命名（entropy_mean, entropy_std, entropy_max, entropy_min）和全文讨论使用 \"entropy\" 指代 per-token NLL。尽管有免责声明，但 entropy 在信息论中有明确定义（Shannon entropy of the full output distribution），与 point-wise NLL 不同。This creates persistent confusion，特别是在讨论 \"distributional shape\" 时——读者可能将 entropy_std 理解为 distribution entropy 的 std，而非 NLL sequence 的 std。\n- 建议：考虑全文改用 \"NLL\" 或 \"surprise\" 以避免歧义，或至少在 Section 3.3 中更明确地区分。\n\n---\n\n## 小问题（不影响接收）\n\n1. **PDF 格式 bug**：第 24 页 Figure 9 caption 下方有游离的 \"chance\" 文字，疑似 LaTeX 排版残留。\n2. **Appendix 数量过多**（20+ 节，pages 13-29）：建议合并相关分析（如 seed control + TOST + ablation → \"Robustness Controls\"），减少到 10-12 个核心 appendix。\n3. **附录中 generator subset 不一致**（main.tex 第40-41行的 note 试图统一说明但不够清晰）：每个 appendix table 应在 caption 中明确标注使用的 generator subset。\n4. **Figure 2 (page 8)** 中 \"Binary F1 (gen-1 vs. gen-2+)\" 标签字体偏小，打印后可读性受限。\n5. **.tex 注释中的 em-dash**（abstract.tex:1, appendix_length.tex:1, main.tex:1）：不影响渲染，但建议清理。\n6. **Reference verification**：67 条 bib 引用，无 orphan/missing。OpenAlex 未找到 7 条（含 DetectGPT, XGBoost, Pythia 等知名论文——likely API lookup issues，非引用问题）。5 条 mismatch（字段差异，需人工确认）。引用数量达到 EMNLP 标准。\n\n---\n\n## 缺失引用\n\n1. **Synthetic data detection in training pipelines**: 近期有较多工作关注 pre-training data 中 synthetic content 的检测和过滤 [如 FineWeb filtering pipeline, Dolma curation]，论文 §2.4 仅引用了 DSIR/QuRating/CCNet，缺少更直接的 synthetic data contamination detection 工作。\n2. **MTLD/HD-D 在 LLM detection 中的应用**: §2.3 提到 TTR 和 hapax \"suffice for depth discrimination\" 并引用 MTLD/HD-D，但未讨论这些更高级指标是否可能提供额外信号。考虑到 TTR 和 hapax 高度相关（r=0.97），替代指标可能有价值。\n3. **Concurrent work on recursive generation quality**: 除 Broken Telephone 和 Markovian Chains 外，是否有 2025-2026 年的其他相关工作关注 iterative/recursive generation 的分布特性？\n\n---\n\n## 主要优势\n\n1. **实验方法论设计严谨**：LOGO cross-validation、length-matched subsampling、independent-seed control、perturbation robustness、seed-level demeaning——每一个潜在 confound 都有对应的 control experiment。这是该论文最大的亮点。\n2. **统计严谨性**：Bootstrap 95% CIs、McNemar tests、TOST equivalence testing、Bonferroni correction、class-balanced subsampling——统计实践远超该领域平均水平。\n3. **诚实透明**：明确承认 base-model scope、moderate performance、temperature sensitivity、negative downstream result。不过度宣传。\n4. **Task formulation 有价值**：从 binary detection 到 depth classification 的扩展是自然但有意义的，开辟了一个新方向。\n5. **QLoRA 实验**（Appendix E）有效验证了 depth signal 的真实性，3-class F1 从 0.789 跳到 0.905。\n6. **Related Work 完整且定位准确**：与 Broken Telephone / Markovian Chains 的对比清晰，不 overstate 自身贡献。\n7. **写作质量良好**：几乎无 AI 写作痕迹（无 em-dash、无 -ing 分词结尾堆砌、分段自然），文本流畅度明显高于平均投稿。\n8. **格式合规**：正文 ≤8 页（Limitations/Ethics 在 page 8-9，不计入限制），匿名、行号、ACL review mode 均正确。Limitations section 详尽。\n\n---\n\n## Framing 一致性评估\n\n- **Abstract → Introduction**：一致，均以 recursive chain 和 depth classification 开篇，scope 限制（base model）在 Abstract 最后一句和 Introduction §1 第15行声明。\n- **Introduction → Method**：贡献声明与 Method 实际内容一致。\n- **Method → Results**：DDR characterization 在 Method 中定义、Results 中验证，逻辑连贯。\n- **Results → Discussion**：Discussion 正确回收 temperature sensitivity 和 instruct degradation 的结果。\n- **标题与内容**：\"Depth Profiling: Classifying Recursive Generation Depth in Base LLM Text Chains\"——标题准确反映内容，\"Base LLM\" 明确 scope。\n\n**Framing 张力**：论文在 \"empirical study\" 和 \"method paper\" 之间摇摆。Introduction 说 \"contributions are empirical\"，但 Table 2 的 baseline comparison 和 inference cost 讨论暗示方法竞争力。Discussion 又回到分析性。这种张力不致命，但使论文的 identity 模糊——审稿人可能从方法论角度批评 novelty 不足，也可能从 empirical study 角度批评 insight 不够深入。建议明确选择一个定位并贯穿全文。\n\n---\n\n## 格式合规性检查\n\n| 检查项 | 状态 | 备注 |\n|--------|------|------|\n| ACL template ([review] mode) | ✓ | `\\usepackage[review]{acl}` |\n| 正文 ≤8 页 | ✓ | 正文至 page 8 底部，Limitations/Ethics 在 pages 8-9（不计入限制）|\n| 行号 | ✓ | `\\linenumbers` 全文可见 |\n| 匿名化 | ✓ | `\\author{Anonymous}`，无自识别信息 |\n| 附录 double-column | ✓/⚠ | 大部分 double-column，部分 figure pages (21-25) 使用 full-width `figure*`（可接受但需注意 2025 年 7 月新规）|\n| Limitations section | ✓ | 详尽（32 行），覆盖所有主要限制 |\n| Ethics statement | ✓ | 完整，含 licenses、compute budget、software versions |\n| Reference 格式 (natbib) | ✓ | `acl_natbib.bst` |\n| Responsible NLP Checklist | — | 未见（ARR 可能在 OpenReview 系统中单独提交）|",

[Round 1 审稿请求]:
## 交叉分享 — 请逐条回应 Reviewer 1 的审稿意见

以下是 Reviewer 1 的完整审稿原文。请逐条回应每个 Must-Fix 和 Should-Fix 问题：
- **[AGREE]**：同意，并说明理由
- **[REBUT]**：反驳，并提供证据（cite 具体 Section/Table/Figure）
  
---

## Reviewer 1 — Round 1 审稿报告

**Overall Assessment**: 3.0 / 5 — Findings
**Confidence**: 4 / 5

### Must-Fix 问题

**N1** [critical] DDR 阈值缺乏先验理论/经验基础，存在 post-hoc 风险
- Cohen's d 阈值（drifting: d>0.75, near-stationary: d<0.10）和 Spearman ρ 阈值（drifting: |ρ|>0.60, near-stationary: |ρ|<0.10）。切分点无理论推导或先验经验支撑。"shifting thresholds by ±0.10 does not alter grouping"（§4.2 L67）仅说明三分对阈值微调稳定，不说明三分是数据中的自然结构。
- 核心担忧：如果三分是 post-hoc 从数据中"读出来"的，DDR 作为 primary contribution 的 novelty 就减弱了。
- 建议：(a) gap statistics / silhouette analysis / clustering evidence 证明三分自然 emerge，(b) 在 held-out 数据集上预注册阈值验证 replicability，(c) 或将 DDR 定位为 descriptive framework。

**N2** [critical] 特征选择过程不透明，存在循环论证风险
- 没说明：(a) 是否存在更大初始特征集？(b) 特征选择在 DDR 分析前还是后？(c) 是否有被排除的特征因不符合三分结构被丢弃？
- entropy_min 特别可疑：LOFO ΔF1=+0.01pp 但保留理由是 "completes the four-number NLL summary"。像是为维持 3/1/3 对称性。
- 建议：明确报告特征选择时间线和过程。

**N3** [critical] 实际应用价值存疑：base model 限制 + 负面 downstream 结果
- (a) Instruct: OLMo-Instruct F1 从 0.763 降至 0.338。(b) Downstream: depth-aware curation 不优于 random（PPL 2.387 vs 2.367, TOST 确认等价）。(c) 两个结果组合意味着方法在实际场景不可用且无实际用途。
- 建议：(a) 重定位为 scientific contribution，弱化 data governance 动机，(b) 或提供 positive downstream application，(c) 添加 instruct 成功案例。

### Should-Fix 问题

**W1** [warning] Table 1 vs Table 2 binary F1 不一致（0.694 vs 0.705），评估协议差异解释不清
- 同方法两表相差 1.1pp。pooled vs macro-average 区别对 reproduce 很关键但未在方法论中明确定义。

**W2** [warning] TTR 和 hapax_ratio 高度冗余（r=0.97），膨胀了 DDR drifting 组计数
- 本质同一个信号。"reducing effective dimensionality to ≈5"（§4.2 L79）但仍计为两个独立 drifting features。

**W3** [warning] Temperature sensitivity 限制普适性声明
- t=0.9 导致所有 4 个 generator 损失 9.3-26.0pp。方法只在 t∈[0.5, 0.7] 可靠，但实际应用中温度未知。

**W4** [warning] Cross-domain 实验使用不同解码参数（t=1.0 vs t=0.7）
- Cross-domain 使用 t=1.0, top-p=1.0，主实验 t=0.7, top-p=0.9。引入 confound。

**W5** [warning] TOST 等价性阈值选择缺乏 application-specific 论证
- ε=|d|<0.20 惯例值，未论证为何适合 depth classification。tail_decay 仅 4/8 generator 通过等价性检验。

**W6** [warning] "entropy" 术语混用
- 用 "entropy" 指代 per-token NLL，与信息论 Shannon entropy 不同。

### 小问题
- M1: Abstract F1=0.694 无 CI，正文 Table 1 有 CI [.688, .700]
- M2: Appendix generator subset 不一致（6-gen vs 8-gen）
- M3: Qualitative example 过于极端，建议增加 subtle 案例
- M4: OLMo temperature 实验使用不同版本模型
- M5: 大量关键分析放在 appendix，建议提升 1-2 个到正文

### Questions
- Q1: 7 特征是否从更大候选集选出？
- Q2: DDR 三分可否通过无监督方法自动 emerge？
- Q3: Qwen/OLMo 温度敏感性差异是否可能是 tokenizer 导致？
- Q4: Cross-model chains 的提升来自 protocol difference 还是 generator mixing？
- Q5: Instruct models 的 "anti-DDR" 模式是否有独立分析价值？

---

请对每个 N1-N3、W1-W6 逐条给出 [AGREE] 或 [REBUT]，附上你的理由和证据。

```text
The Review Director is asking me (Reviewer 2) to respond to each of Reviewer 1's Must-Fix and Should-Fix issues with [AGREE] or [REBUT]. Let me go through each one carefully, drawing on my own reading of the paper.

Let me think through each issue:

**N1: DDR thresholds are post-hoc**
I largely agree with this. The thresholds are indeed data-derived. The paper says "shifting thresholds by ±0.10 does not alter grouping" but R1 is right that this doesn't prove the partition is natural structure. However, my own review noted DDR as "descriptive rather than explanatory" which is similar but from a different angle. 

I should agree but acknowledge that the TOST per-generator analysis and the convergence of multiple metrics across different statistical tests do provide some empirical support for the partition, even if the threshold selection itself remains somewhat post-hoc.

**N2: Feature selection process opaque, circular reasoning risk**
This is a fair critique I didn't fully develop in my own review. The paper doesn't clearly explain whether these 7 features were selected from a larger set, and the entropy_min justification does read as post-hoc rationalization. I should agree with this concern.

**N3: Practical applic

**

I strongly agree—this mirrors my own N2 and W1. The limited scope to base models combined with negative downstream results is genuinely problematic.

**W1: Table 1 vs Table 2 inconsistency**
I agree—I flagged this as W2 in my own review regarding the pooled versus macro-average confusion.

**W2: TTR and hapax_ratio redundancy**
Valid point. With r=0.97, these features are nearly identical, yet the paper treats them as independent drifting features, which artificially inflates the 3/1/3 partition. I should agree.

**W3: Temperature sensitivity**
I agree—I raised this as W3 in my own review.

**W4: Cross-domain different decoding parameters**
This is a legitimate concern. The cross-domain experiment uses different temperature and top-p settings than the main experiment, introducing a confound that makes it hard to isolate what's driving the performance differences.

**W5: TOST threshold justification**
The ε=|d|<0.20 threshold follows Cohen's convention, but R1 is right that applying it here without domain-specific justification is weak. Especially since tail_decay only passes for 4 out of 8 generators. I should partially agree.

**W6: "entropy" terminology**
I agree—I raised this as W5 in my own review.

Now I'll draft my response.
```

alter grouping\", §4.2 L67）仅证明 partition 对微调稳定，不证明它是数据中的自然结构。我自己的 N1 也从另一角度指出了同一问题：DDR 本质上是描述性分类而非有理论支撑的发现。\n\n补充两点细微差异：\n\n(1) 论文确实使用了多个独立度量的收敛来支撑 partition（ρ、Cohen's d、KS statistic、Levene test、CV shift，§4.2 L73），且 per-generator TOST（Appendix T, Table）显示 entropy_min 在 8/8 generators 通过等价性检验、entropy_max 在 6/8 通过，而所有 drifting features 在 0/8 通过。这比单一阈值的 post-hoc 分类要强，但 R1 的核心论点仍然成立：没有证据表明\"三分\"优于\"两分\"或\"连续谱\"。\n\n(2) R1 建议的 gap statistics / silhouette analysis 非常到位。如果在 7 维特征空间中对 (ρ, d) 对做 clustering，能否自动 recover 三分结构？这是可验证的。若只 recover 两组（drifting vs. stationary），intermediate 类别（仅 entropy_std 一个成员）的存在理由就更薄弱了。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-N2: 特征选择过程不透明，存在循环论证风险**\n\n同意，这个问题我自己审稿时未充分展开。关键疑点：\n\n(a) §3.3 第32-36行解释特征选择动机为 \"interpretable distributional statistics rather than learned representations so that the DDR tri-partition remains transparent\"。这隐含地承认特征选择服务于 DDR 叙事。如果 DDR 是 primary contribution，而特征是为了展示 DDR 而选择的，那确实存在循环论证风险。\n\n(b) R1 对 entropy_min 的观察非常敏感。§3.3 第49-50行的保留理由（\"LOFO ΔF1=+0.01pp, within bootstrap noise\" + \"completes the four-number NLL summary\"）读起来确实像 post-hoc rationalization。一个对分类无贡献的特征被保留，最合理的解释是它在 DDR 叙事中有位置价值（充实 near-stationary 组），这正是 R1 指出的 3/1/3 对称性问题。\n\n(c) 论文未说明是否测试过其他候选特征（如 MTLD、HD-D、n-gram entropy、repetition rate）。§2.3 提到 \"more sophisticated alternatives exist (MTLD, HD-D; MATTR) but TTR and hapax ratio suffice for depth discrimination\"，但 \"suffice\" 是事后判断，不是先验选择。\n\n建议与 R1 一致：需要明确的特征选择时间线和完整候选集报告。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-N3: 实际应用价值存疑**\n\n完全同意。这与我自己的 N2（base-model scope）和 W1（negative downstream result）高度一致。\n\n补充一个 R1 未展开的角度：即使将论文重定位为 scientific contribution（R1 建议 a），DDR 作为 scientific finding 的深度也不足——因为没有理论解释为什么 location drift 而 shape stable（我的 N1）。所以论文同时面临两个方向的困境：作为方法论文缺乏实用性，作为科学发现缺乏理论深度。\n\nR1 建议 (c)（添加 instruct 成功案例）是最有建设性的方向。如果能找到 instruct model 上 DDR 的变体形式（即使 pattern 不同但仍可分类），可以同时解决 applicability 和 novelty 问题。§5 第10行提到 \"consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than destroying the iterative dynamics\"，这暗示 instruct 下可能存在不同的但可利用的 depth signal。\n\n---\n\n### Should-Fix 问题\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W1: Table 1 vs Table 2 binary F1 不一致（0.694 vs 0.705）**\n\n同意，我也独立提出了这个问题（我的 W2）。Table 1 的 0.694 是 per-generator binary F1 的 macro average（8 个 generator 各自的 binary F1 取平均），Table 2 的 0.705 是 pooled evaluation（所有 generator 的预测合并后计算 binary F1）。Table 1 caption 注释了 \"binary F1 is leak-free\"，Table 2 caption 注释了 \"pooled evaluation; slightly higher than per-generator macro average in Table 1\"，但这一关键方法论差异应在 §3.4 (Evaluation Protocol) 中正式定义，而非依赖 caption 注释。同一方法在同一论文中的两个 primary metric 不一致，会严重影响可信度。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W2: TTR 和 hapax_ratio 高度冗余（r=0.97）**\n\n同意。r=0.97 意味着二者携带本质相同的信号。§4.2 L79 承认 \"reducing effective dimensionality to ≈5\"，但仍在 DDR partition 中将二者计为两个独立 drifting features，使 drifting 组从 2 个膨胀到 3 个（entropy_mean + TTR/hapax 实质上只有 2 个独立信号）。如果 drifting 组实际只有 2 个独立特征，partition 变成 2/1/3，\"三分\" 的结构对称性就不那么compelling了。\n\n论文的辩护（\"both are retained for marginal LOFO gains\", §4.2 L79）是分类器性能的理由，不是 DDR 结构的理由。分类器中保留冗余特征是合理的，但在 DDR 叙事中将冗余特征计为独立成员是 misleading。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W3: Temperature sensitivity 限制普适性声明**\n\n完全同意，我也独立提出（我的 W3）。补充：不仅 t=0.9 时信号衰减，温度敏感性还是 model-dependent 的（Pythia/OLMo 在 t=0.5 获益 +7-9pp，Qwen 几乎不变），这进一步说明 DDR 不是 universal property of recursive generation，而是 parameter × model family 的交互效应。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W4: Cross-domain 实验使用不同解码参数**\n\n同意。Appendix J 明确说明 cross-domain 使用 \"t=1.0, top-p=1.0\"（第24行），与主实验的 t=0.7, top-p=0.9 不同。论文将此解释为 \"domain × temperature interaction\"（第25-27行），但更准确的说法是：cross-domain 结果和 main 结果在不同解码设置下获得，因此不能直接比较。特别是 cross-domain binary F1 > 0.73 的结论是在 t=1.0 下获得的，而 W3 已经指出主实验在 t=0.9 时大幅衰减。这两个结果放在一起是矛盾的：为什么 main experiment 在 t=0.9 崩溃，但 cross-domain 在 t=1.0 却表现良好？论文给出的解释（reference model calibration）合理但未经验证。\n\n**[REBUT] R1-W5: TOST 等价性阈值选择缺乏 application-specific 论证**\n\n部分反驳。ε=|d|<0.20 并非任意选择，而是 Cohen (1988) 定义的 \"small effect\" 标准阈值，Lakens (2017) 推荐用于等价性检验。在没有 domain-specific 先验时使用该惯例值是方法论上可接受的。论文也在 Appendix L (downstream) 中展示了 margin sensitivity analysis（Table, testing ±1%/2%/3%/5%），证明结论对阈值选择不敏感。\n\n但 R1 指出的 tail_decay 仅 4/8 generator 通过这一点是有效的。Appendix T Table 显示 tail_decay 的 |d| 范围为 0.07-0.22，其中 4 个 generator 超过 0.15，说明 tail_decay 的 near-stationary 分类并不稳健。这削弱了 DDR partition 的一致性，与 R1-N1 的 post-hoc 风险呼应。\n\n综合判断：阈值选择本身可辩护，但 tail_decay 的不一致性是实质问题。\n\n**[AGREE] R1-W6: \"entropy\" 术语混用**\n\n完全同意，我也独立提出（我的 W5）。特别在讨论 \"distributional shape\" 时，entropy_std 容易被误解为 distribution entropy 的标准差（衡量 output distribution 的离散程度如何波动），而实际是 per-token NLL sequence 的标准差（衡量 token-level surprise 的变异性）。这两者含义差异大。\n\n---\n\n### 对 R1 小问题的简要回应\n\n- **M1 (Abstract 无 CI)**：同意，Abstract 空间有限但可加 \"±0.006\" 或在 Introduction 首次引用时附 CI。\n- **M2 (Appendix generator subset 不一致)**：同意，我也独立提出。\n- **M3 (Qualitative examples 过于极端)**：合理建议，但优先级低。\n- **M4 (OLMo temperature 版本差异)**：注意到 Appendix H footnote 已标注 \"OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release)\"，但确实引入了不可控变量。\n- **M5 (关键分析放 appendix)**：强烈同意。QLoRA 结果（3-class F1: 0.789→0.905）和 negative downstream result 都应在正文 Discussion 中更显著地讨论。这与我的 N3（贡献列表过薄）和 W1（negative downstream 被埋）一致。\n\n---\n\n### 综合评估\n\nR1 和我（R2）的审稿意见高度一致：\n\n| 议题 | R1 | R2 | 判断 |\n|------|----|----|------|\n| DDR 新颖性/理论深度 | N1 (post-hoc 风险) | N1 (描述性非解释性) | 同一问题不同角度 |\n| 实际应用限制 | N3 (base + downstream) | N2 + W1 | 完全一致 |\n| 贡献厚度 | 隐含在 N1-N3 | N3 (explicit) | 一致 |\n| Table 1/2 不一致 | W1 | W2 | 完全一致 |\n| Temperature sensitivity | W3 | W3 | 完全一致 |\n| Entropy 术语 | W6 | W5 | 完全一致 |\n| 特征选择透明度 | N2 | 未充分展开 | 补充同意 |\n| TTR/hapax 冗余 | W2 | 未明确提出 | 补充同意 |\n| Cross-domain 参数差异 | W4 | 未明确提出 | 补充同意 |\n\n两位 reviewer 独立给出 Overall 3.0 (Findings)、Confidence 4/5，且核心问题几乎完全重叠，这增强了评估的可靠性。主要分歧仅在 TOST 阈值问题上（我认为阈值本身可辩护，但 tail_decay 的不一致性是实质问题）。\n\n**修改优先级建议（若作者 revise）**：\n1. 最高优先：解决 base-model scope（至少 2-3 个 instruct model 的系统实验）\n2. 高优先：明确特征选择时间线，报告是否有被排除的候选特征\n3. 高优先：为 DDR 提供理论解释或重定位为 descriptive framework\n4. 中优先：统一评估协议（消除 Table 1/2 差异，统一 generator subset）\n5. 中优先：将 QLoRA 和 negative downstream 结果提升到正文",
```json
  "reviewer_id": "2",
  "project": "gen-depth-fingerprint"
}
```
